| 2026-10-14 | 2.3 | How the UUIDv7 generator draws from a 32-bit `RandomSource`, and what "the same ids as Android" means | `RandomSource` gives 32-bit words (step 2.1), Android's generator a Kotlin `nextLong()` for the counter seed and a `nextInt()` for the tail. The web generator draws, per new millisecond or overflow, two words for the seed, the high word first (the order of Kotlin's `nextLong()`), masked to the seed's 41 bits word by word, then one word for the tail; the words stay below 2^32 and the counter below 2^42 as `Number`s, split by division and modulo. The Android tests are property tests without fixed ids (as iOS log 2.3 found), so the exact vectors were made from the Android code itself: `packages/core/test/util/android-uuid-v7-vectors.jsh` runs the compiled Android `UuidV7Generator` and test `SeededRandom` (rubicon-android-sdk `2e1e8b1`, unchanged at `99f27a4`) under `jshell` on three scenarios of `UuidV7GeneratorTest` (seed 9 at clock 0; seed 1 at 1 759 147 200 123 through a later millisecond and a clock set back a day; the 3-bit counter that overflows), and records the ids with the words of the `Long` and `Int` values the generator received. Kotlin's `nextLong()` adds the low `nextInt()` sign-extended, so a recorded high word can be one less than the raw draw; recording the received values keeps the web generator free of that Kotlin detail. Replayed through `ScriptedRandom` (new in `rubicon-analytics-testing`, the iOS name; it throws when its words run out), the web generator gives the 53 Android ids exactly. The iOS vector (`0192e4a1-7c3f-7000-8000-0005deadbeef`) is kept as well. | - |

The Android `daysFromCivil` is ported for the HTTP date parser, since `Date.UTC` reads the years 0 to 99 as 1900 to 1999; the Android `civilFromDays` and `format` arithmetic are ported into the test as the reference for 100 000 random instants and the day round trip. Proven by the Android `Hashing` vectors, the NIST examples (`"abc"`, the empty string, the two-block 448-bit and 896-bit messages and a million `a`), Node's `crypto` on every length from 0 to 200 bytes and on 2000 random strings of any UTF-16 code unit, and non-ASCII input (the Android Turkish key, two- and three-byte characters, surrogate pairs, U+10FFFF). | 2026-10-14 | 2.5 | How the rows are told apart (Web Serialization 2: `Object.prototype.toString`, never `instanceof`) | The `Object.prototype.toString` tag picks the row and a check on the internal slot through this realm's built-ins confirms it, so a value from any frame qualifies and one that only claims a tag falls through to the next row: boxed primitives by `Number`, `String`, `Boolean` and `BigInt` `.prototype.valueOf.call`; `Set` and `Map` by their `forEach.call`; typed arrays by the `%TypedArray%` prototype's tag and `length` getters through `Reflect.get` with the value as receiver (a `Proxy` of one is not one); a `Date` through `formatIsoDate` of step 2.3; a plain object by its prototype: `null`, or a prototype whose own prototype is `null` and whose own `constructor` is a native `Object`, compared by `Function.prototype.toString` text, which is the same in every frame; `Array.isArray`. | 2026-10-15 | 2.5 | Huge lists and containers (reading h of 2026-10-14, changed by the user on 2026-10-15; a web difference from Android, which walks a list whole) | The user's decision: `track('x', { a: new Array(1e9) })` returns at once instead of freezing the page, and the event is dropped as `oversize` exactly as the whole walk would drop it; nothing changes below the limit. `normalizeProperties(input, stopAboveBytes = MAX_EVENT_BYTES)` counts, while it copies, the UTF-8 bytes of the copy's JSON (exact for `null`, booleans, numbers, and keys and strings without escapes; a lower bound with escapes) and stops every list, set, map and object walk once the count passes the limit; `NormalizedProperties.oversize` is then `true` (only tests pass another limit, `Infinity` for the whole walk). The count holds only what stays in the copy: a value under a key that a later key of the same object replaces (a lone surrogate replaced, or a `Proxy` listing a key twice) is walked uncounted, and a value dropped as unreadable takes its count back. So `oversize: true` means the whole walk's properties are over 32 KB too, and `false` means nothing was cut and the result is the whole walk's. The normalizer drops nothing: the cut copy is itself over the limit, so the writer's scan (6.3b) drops the event as `oversize` with the same reason, counter and `EVENT_OVERSIZE` line even if the flag is lost (stored super properties); for that the line names no byte count, unlike Android's. Arrays and typed arrays are read element by element as the walk reaches them; sets and maps are still collected first as plain lists of values (the engine caps them at 2^24 entries), so a getter that changes one during the walk changes nothing; the depth check reads a set's or map's slot through its `size` getter instead of copying it. `mergeSuperProperties` returns the new `MergedProperties` (no flag). Not identical for an oversize input, as stopping implies: problems after the stop are neither logged nor counted, getters after the stop do not run, and keys after the stop are missing from the cut copy, which a `beforeSend` hook would see and `register` would store (an event whose own key replaces the cut super property would then lack the super properties after it). Open, for those steps to ask: 6.3b, whether the writer honours the flag before or after the hooks (recommended: after, so a hook's `null`, throw or violation keeps its reason, and the event is dropped as `oversize` when either pass is flagged); 6.1, what `register` does with a flagged result (recommended: store the cut copy). | 2026-10-15 | 4.2 | Readings of the pages for the chunk store | (a) The capacity sums this key's chunks and markers only: another key's chunks are another project's and are never trimmed (Web Disk 6). (c) The value follows Android 3: every line ends with `\n`; a reader drops one empty last piece and tolerates a missing final break, and an empty line inside is corrupt. (d) The store splits a write into chunks of at most 100 events whose lines, joined by commas inside an envelope of the longest `sent_at`, fit the 48 KiB keepalive budget (Web Queue 5), so no chunk exceeds it whatever the writer gives; a single line always makes a chunk. (f) `UNKNOWN_FILE` names the number of keys deleted, never a key, whose text another script may have written. (g) A batch in flight is never trimmed or expired by this page's walk (Android log 6.7); another page's in-flight batch cannot be seen, the over-count Web Queue 6 accepts. (h) A walk can miss a key when another page deletes one meanwhile (the indexes of `key(i)` shift); the next walk finds it. | - |

(e) A 304 to the GET is `success` with status 304 and its `ETag` if exposed (Web HTTP 3), where Android returns `HttpError(304)` (Android log 5.1). (e) "Counted" is every group B result with an HTTP status (408, 5xx, 1xx, non-standard, status 0), which `fetch` reports only for a real response. (h) The early attempt of Web Retry 2 is offered only while a transient wait after `connect`, `io` or `timeout` is in force; without one the `online` flush trigger goes through the gate. (e) Chromium and Firefox send a POST again on their own when the server drops its connection, so the server's count of requests is not the SDK's count of attempts; the early-attempt test counts attempts from the SDK's DEBUG lines. (b) A page loaded hidden sends the load's view at its first `visible` (or a restore), with the moment and URL then; a history change before that updates the URL compared with and sends nothing ("a page never shown produces none"). (c) A view always carries its own URL: `page.url`, `page.query` and the `utm_*` come from the URL of its history entry, the rest of the context, its time and its visibility from the moment it is made (the window closing, the next `pushState` or `popstate`, a hiding or `pagehide`). A change of query or fragment only keeps the pending view's URL, so a campaign stripped by the application right after a navigation is kept (Web Lifecycle 5). (d) A `replaceState` that changes the path with no pending view opens a window of its own, like a push, so a redirect chain after it collapses into one view. (e) Timers are hints: a window that has passed by the clock is closed at the next navigation or when the page becomes visible, before anything else is done. (f) A restore from the back/forward cache sends a pending view first, then its own view at once, not through a window. (g) The visibility of a view is the platform's at the moment it is made; on `pagehide`, which arrives while the page is still `visible` in every engine (step 1.4), it is a visible event. (h) The registry is installed at the instance's start, when the configuration the page runs with is known (Web Lifecycle 8: a saved `autocapture_off` of `page_views` wraps nothing at that load), not inside `createAnalytics`. | `packages/web/CLAUDE.md` (Page views) |

A tab that died without `pagehide` is a tab closed after its marker was removed from another page of the origin (Chromium and WebKit run `pagehide` on close, step 1.4). Waits are conditions on the server or the page's log lines (`Uploads wait (throttle)`, `(probe)`, the device id lines), never sleeps; the probe is held in flight by the server's `delayMs`. ## 16. Out of scope for the first release

Recorded on the Confluence pages as rejected or deferred; not implemented until decided: a published
`core` package and a React or React Native package (Web Public API 7); UMD (Web Public API 7); a
Promise from `flush()` (Web Public API 3); an IndexedDB event store (Web Disk 1); `sendBeacon` (Web
Queue 3); a route-template mechanism for `page.url` (Web Context 2); declared clicks, form events, a
page-leave event, `appinstalled`, the Navigation API and an axios interceptor helper (Web Lifecycle
2, 6, 7); `privacy.pendingStorage` and `privacy.respectGpc` (Web Privacy 1, 4); per-page diagnostics
counter keys (Web Logging 5); Web Locks or `BroadcastChannel` for the two Session races (entry page,
open questions); ad-blocker evasion (Web HTTP 5); a Trusted Types policy (Web HTTP 6). The Android
and iOS lists apply as well: wrapper SDK boundaries, SDK lifecycle states and `shutdown()` in the
public API, performance and reliability budgets, the versioned backend contract document and
deduplication window, a diagnostic API beyond `SDK Diagnostics`, typed property overloads, `group`.
